2. For each iteration `nl` from `0` to `iterations-1`:  
   - Find max value in `a` and its index.  
   - Compute `chksum = max_value + index` (but `chksum` is overwritten each iteration, not used later).  
3. Return `index + x + 1` after the last iteration.  

Important: `index` and `x` after the last iteration correspond to the **last iteration’s** found max and its index (since they are overwritten each iteration).  

---

**Example 1:** `LEN_1D = 5`, `iterations = 1`  

Initialize:  
i=0: a[0] = 0  
i=1: a[1] = 7%5 = 2  
i=2: a[2] = 14%5 = 4  
i=3: a[3] = 21%5 = 1  
i=4: a[4] = 28%5 = 3  

Array: [0, 2, 4, 1, 3]  

Find max:  
x starts at a[0]=0, index=0  
i=0: a[0]=0 ≤ 0 → no change  
i=1: 2 > 0 → x=2, index=1  
i=2: 4 > 2 → x=4, index=2  
i=3: 1 ≤ 4 → no  
i=4: 3 ≤ 4 → no  

Final: x=4, index=2  
Return: index + x + 1 = 2 + 4 + 1 = 7.0  

---

**Example 2:** `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 2`  

Initialize:  
i=0: 0%3=0  
i=1: 7%3=1  
i=2: 14%3=2  

Array: [0, 1, 2]  

First iteration:  
Find max: x=2, index=2  

Second iteration:  
Same array → same max: x=2, index=2  

Return: 2 + 2 + 1 = 5.0  

---

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

```c
float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Initialize array (independent, can be vectorized)
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    // Outer loop remains unchanged
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        x = a[0];
        index = 0;
        
        // Split max-finding into two phases to remove branching
        // Phase 1: find max value (vectorizable)
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            // Use conditional move logic without branching
            int cond = val > x;
            x = cond ? val : x;
        }
        
        // Phase 2: find index of max value (vectorizable with masking)
        // Reset index for safety
        index = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            // Compare each element with max value
            int cond = a[i] == x;
            // Update index if match (last match wins, matching original)
            index = cond ? i : index;
        }
    }
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

## **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original code in the inner loop does:

```c
if (a[i] > x) {
    x = a[i];
    index = i;
}
```

This updates `index` to the **first occurrence** of the maximum value (since it only updates when `a[i] > x`, not when `a[i] == x` after the max is found).

The optimized code splits into two phases:

1. Find max value `x` (vectorizable, no branching).
2. Find index of `x` by checking `a[i] == x`, updating `index` if match — **last match wins**.

This is **semantically different** when there are duplicate max values.

---

### **Example to show difference**

Let `LEN_1D = 5`, `iterations = 1`.

Initialize:  
`a[i] = (i*7) % 5`  
i=0: 0  
i=1: 2  
i=2: 4  
i=3: 1  
i=4: 3  

Max value = 4 at i=2.

Both original and optimized return `index = 2`, `x = 4`, final result = `2 + 4 + 1 = 7`. Works.

---

### **Example with duplicate max values**

Let `LEN_1D = 4`:  
`a[i] = (i*7) % 4`  
i=0: 0  
i=1: 3  
i=2: 2  
i=3: 1  

Max = 3 at i=1 only. Works.

---

### **Example where duplicates matter**

We need duplicates of the max value in array.  
But initialization `(i*7) % LEN_1D` can produce duplicates only if `LEN_1D` shares factors with 7? Actually 7 is prime, so `(i*7) % LEN_1D` cycles through all residues mod LEN_1D if gcd(LEN_1D,7)=1, so no duplicates except 0? Wait, check small LEN_1D:

`LEN_1D = 7`:  
`(i*7) % 7 = 0` for all i.  
All values are 0. Max = 0.

Original loop:  
Start x = a[0] = 0, index=0.  
i=0: a[0]=0 not > 0 → no update.  
i=1: a[1]=0 not > 0 → no update.  
…  
index stays 0.

Optimized:  
Phase1: x stays 0.  
Phase2: find index where a[i]==0, last match is i=6 → index=6.

**Different result**.

Final return:  
Original: `0 + 0 + 1 = 1`  
Optimized: `6 + 0 + 1 = 7`  
**Semantic mismatch**.

---

Thus, **semantics are inconsistent** due to duplicate max values case.

---

## **3. Conclusion**

The optimized code changes behavior when there are duplicate maximums in the array.  
Since the initialization can produce duplicates (e.g., LEN_1D=7), the unit test analysis is correct: semantics differ.

We must stop here.

            // Compare each element with max value
            int cond = a[i] == x;
            // Update index if match (last match wins, matching original)
            index = cond ? i : index;
        }
    }
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original code in the inner loop does:

```c

    index = i;
}
```

This updates `index` to the **first occurrence** of the maximum value (since it only updates when `a[i] > x`, not when `a[i] == x` after the max is found).

The optimized code splits into two phases:

1. Find max value `x` (vectorizable, no branching).
2. Find index of `x` by checking `a[i] == x`, updating `index` if match — **last match wins**.

This is **semantically different** when there are duplicate max values.

Compiler feedback says:  
- First loop (initialization) vectorized.  
- Second loop (max finding) **not vectorized**: “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop.”  

The issue: `index` is not a simple reduction variable; it’s the *position* of the max, not the max itself.  
Standard max reduction can be vectorized (using `max` across lanes), but tracking the index where the max occurred across SIMD lanes is trickier — each lane may have its own candidate index, and at the end you must pick the one corresponding to the global max.  

The compiler cannot automatically vectorize this because the reduction pattern for `index` is not recognized.

We can restructure to compute max and index in a vector-friendly way:  
- Find max value via vectorized max reduction (possible with intrinsics or letting compiler do it if we separate logic).  
- Then do a single pass to find the index of that max (or keep track during reduction manually with SIMD).  

But the current optimized code still has the same non-vectorizable pattern as the original for `index`.  

Since the compiler says it’s not vectorized, and there is an optimization opportunity (splitting the loop: first find max, then find index of max), we should mark **FAIL** here.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

  - The original loop tracks both `x` and `index` together, but `index` is only updated when a new max is found, which corresponds to the **first occurrence** of the running maximum.  
  - The optimized version finds the global max first, then finds its first occurrence. This yields the same `x` and `index` as the original.  

Let’s test with a small example manually:

**Example 1:**  
`LEN_1D = 5`, `iterations = 1`.  
Initialization:  
`a = [0, 2, 4, 1, 3]` (since `(i*7)%5` yields `0,2,4,1,3`).  

Original:  
- `x = 0`, `index = 0`.  
- i=1: `2 > 0` → `x=2`, `index=1`.  
- i=2: `4 > 2` → `x=4`, `index=2`.  
- i=3: no. i=4: no.  
Result: `x=4`, `index=2`.  

Optimized:  
- Phase 1: `max_val = 4`.  
- Phase 2: first index where `a[i]==4` is `i=2`.  
Result: `x=4`, `index=2`. ✅

**Example 2:**  
`LEN_1D = 4`, `a = [0, 3, 2, 1]` (since `(i*7)%4` = `0,3,2,1`).  

Original:  
- i=0: x=0, index=0.  
- i=1: 3>0 → x=3, index=1.  
- i=2: no, i=3: no.  
Result: x=3, index=1.

Optimized:  
- max_val = 3.  
- first index of 3 is i=1. ✅

**Example 3:** Multiple equal maxima case:  
`a = [5, 3, 5, 2]`.  

Original:  
- i=0: x=5, index=0.  
- i=1: no.  
- i=2: 5 > 5? No (strictly greater), so index stays 0.  
- i=3: no.  
Result: x=5, index=0.

Optimized:  
- max_val = 5.  
- first index of 5 is i=0. ✅

Semantics match.

---

## **3. Vectorization Analysis (Optimized Code Only)**

Compiler feedback:  
- First initialization loop: vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 1).  
- Phase 1 (max reduction): vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 4). ✅  
- Phase 2 (first index of max_val): **not vectorized** because “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop”.  

The issue: Phase 2 loop has early exit condition via `found` flag, and `index` is conditionally updated. This is not a pure reduction; it’s a search loop with a condition that breaks vectorization because later iterations depend on `found` from earlier iterations (loop-carried dependency).

---

**Why vectorization fails for Phase 2:**  
The loop has:  
```c
int cond = (a[i] == max_val) && !found;
index = cond ? i : index;
found = found || cond;
```
`found` is a loop-carried dependency (iteration i+1 depends on iteration i’s `found`). This prevents auto-vectorization.

---

**Optimization opportunity:**  
We can make Phase 2 vectorizable by removing the `found` dependency:  
- First, find *any* index where `a[i] == max_val` using vectorized comparison.  
- Then, if multiple matches, pick smallest index.  
But “first occurrence” requires knowing the smallest i where condition is true, which is a min reduction over indices where condition holds. That’s still a reduction but with a mask. It can be vectorized as:  
```
first_index = large_number;
for i in 0..n-1:
    if a[i] == max_val:
        first_index = min(first_index, i);
```
This is a min reduction with a mask, which is vectorizable.

Current code’s `found` flag is unnecessary for correctness if we compute `first_index` as above.  
Thus, the current Phase 2 is suboptimal and fails vectorization — this is an **optimization opportunity missed**.

---

Since the compiler says Phase 2 is not vectorized and there is a clear way to rewrite it to be vectorizable (using min reduction), the optimized code is **not fully optimized**.

---

## **4. Conclusion**

The optimized code is semantically correct but fails to vectorize Phase 2 due to a loop-carried dependency that can be eliminated. Therefore, it does not meet the requirement of being “fully optimized” (no further modification needed).

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body

     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

### **3. Vectorization Analysis**
Compiler feedback shows:
- First initialization loop: vectorized.
- Phase 1 max reduction loop: vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 4).
- Phase 2 first-index loop: **not vectorized** due to “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop.”

**Why Phase 2 not vectorized:**

     a[i] = x + y;

            first_index = (mask && (i < first_index)) ? i : first_index;
        }
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;

     b[i] = y * z;

     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

## Analysis  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  

The original code finds the **maximum value** in `a` and the **first index** where that maximum occurs, then computes `chksum = x + (float)index` (though `chksum` is unused).  
The optimized code splits this into two loops:  
1. Find the maximum value (reduction).  
2. Find the first index where `a[i] == max_val`.  

This is **semantically equivalent** to the original code because:  
- In the original loop, when `a[i] > x`, both `x` and `index` are updated, so `index` ends up being the **first** occurrence of the maximum (since `>` updates only on strictly greater values, not equal values).  
- In the optimized version, `first_index` is set to the first `i` where `a[i] == max_val`, which matches the original behavior.  

**Test case verification** (small example):  
Let `LEN_1D = 5`, `iterations = 1`.  
Initialization: `a = [0, 2, 4, 1, 3]`.  
Original:  
- Start `x = 0`, `index = 0`.  
- i=1: `2 > 0` → `x=2`, `index=1`.  
- i=2: `4 > 2` → `x=4`, `index=2`.  
- i=3: no update.  
- i=4: no update.  
Result: `x=4`, `index=2`.  

Optimized:  
- Phase 1: `max_val = 4`.  
- Phase 2: first index where `a[i]==4` is `i=2`.  
Matches.  

Thus, semantics are correct.  

---

### 3. Vectorization Analysis (Optimized Code Only)  

Compiler feedback:  
- Loop 1 (initialization) vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 1).  
- Loop 2 (max reduction) vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 4).  
- Loop 3 (first index search) **not vectorized** because:  
  > remark: loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop  

Reason: The first-index search loop has an early exit condition (`first_index == -1`), which is a **loop-carried dependency** (value of `first_index` changes during iteration and affects whether to set it). This prevents vectorization.  

**Optimization opportunity**:  
The first-index search can be vectorized by removing the early exit and instead scanning all indices, but keeping the first match via a min operation on indices where `a[i] == max_val`.  
Example:  
```c
int first_index = LEN_1D; // large
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    if (a[i] == max_val && i < first_index) {
        first_index = i;
    }
}
```  
But this still has a loop-carried dependency on `first_index`.  
Better: Use two vectorized loops:  
1. Vectorized comparison to produce a mask where `a[i] == max_val`.  
2. Find minimum index from mask.  

However, the compiler may still not auto-vectorize due to the conditional min reduction.  
We can rewrite to make it friendly:  
```c
int first_index = LEN_1D;
#pragma omp simd reduction(min:first_index)
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    if (a[i] == max_val) {
        first_index = first_index < i ? first_index : i;
    }
}
if (first_index == LEN_1D) first_index = 0; // fallback
```  
But this requires OpenMP SIMD or compiler hints.  

Given the current optimized code, the **third loop is not vectorized**, so there is **optimization opportunity** left.  

---

### 4. Conclusion  
The optimized code is **semantically correct** but **not fully optimized** because one loop fails to vectorize.  
Thus, it does **not** meet the requirement of being "fully optimized with no further modification needed."

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;

    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:

   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Source code: 
```c
float s315(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++)
        a[i] = (i * 7) % LEN_1D;

    float x, chksum;
    int index;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        x = a[0];
        index = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            if (a[i] > x) {
                x = a[i];
                index = i;
            }
        }
        chksum = x + (float) index;
    }
    return index + x + 1;
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Initialize array (vectorizable)
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        x = a[0];
        index = 0;
        
        // Phase 1: Find max value (vectorizable reduction)
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Find first index of max_val (vectorizable via min reduction)
        int first_index = LEN_1D;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            if (a[i] == max_val) {
                first_index = (i < first_index) ? i : first_index;
            }
        }
        if (first_index == LEN_1D) first_index = 0;
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

```

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  
The original code finds the **maximum value** and its **first occurring index** in each iteration.  
The optimized code splits this into:  
1. Find `max_val` (vectorizable reduction).  
2. Find the **first index** where `a[i] == max_val`.  

However, there is a **semantic bug** in the optimized code:  

**Original**:  
- `x = a[0]; index = 0;`  
- If `a[i] > x`, update `x` and `index`.  
- This means if multiple elements have the same maximum value, `index` is the **first** occurrence (since `>` only triggers on strictly greater values).  

**Optimized**:  
- `max_val` is found correctly.  
- In Phase 2, it tries to find the first index where `a[i] == max_val` using a branchless min reduction:  
  ```c
  int mask = (a[i] == max_val);
  int candidate = mask ? i : first_index;
  first_index = (candidate < first_index) ? candidate : first_index;
  ```
  This works **only if** `first_index` is initialized to a large number (`LEN_1D`).  
  But there’s a problem: if `max_val` never appears (impossible here), `first_index` stays `LEN_1D` and is reset to `0`, which is wrong.  
  Actually, `max_val` always exists, so `first_index` will be set correctly.  

But wait — the **real bug** is that in the original code, if all elements are equal to `a[0]`, then `index` stays `0`.  
In the optimized code, if all elements are equal, `max_val = a[0]`, Phase 2 will find `first_index = 0` (since `a[0] == max_val`).  
So that matches.  

Let’s test with a small example:  
`LEN_1D = 5`, `a = [3, 4, 4, 2, 1]`  
Original:  
- i=0: x=3, index=0  
- i=1: a[1]=4 > 3 → x=4, index=1  
- i=2: a[2]=4 not > 4 → no change  
Result: x=4, index=1  

Optimized:  
- max_val = 4  
- Phase 2: first index where a[i]==4 is i=1 → index=1 ✅  

Another: `a = [5, 5, 5, 5, 5]`  
Original: never updates index (stays 0) → index=0  
Optimized: first index where a[i]==5 is i=0 → index=0 ✅  

So the logic matches.  

But there’s a **floating-point equality** issue:  
`a[i] == max_val` compares floats exactly. Since `a[i]` is computed as `(i*7)%LEN_1D` and stored as float, and `max_val` is also a float from the same array, bitwise equality holds. So safe here.  

Thus, semantics are preserved.  

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Compiler feedback:  
- First initialization loop: vectorized.  
- Phase 1 (max reduction): vectorized (width 4, interleaved count 4).  
- Phase 2 (first index of max_val): **not vectorized** because “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop.”  

The issue: Phase 2 is not a pure reduction; it depends on `max_val` from Phase 1, but the compiler sees `first_index` being updated in a way it can’t recognize as a reduction (due to the conditional masked min pattern).  

**Why not vectorized?**  
The loop has a recurrence:  
`first_index = (a[i]==max_val && i < first_index) ? i : first_index`  
This is a **min reduction with a mask**, but the mask depends on a float comparison that may prevent the compiler from recognizing it as a reduction.  

**Optimization opportunity**:  
We can restructure Phase 2 to be vectorizable:  
- Use a flag to record first occurrence:  
  ```
  int found = 0;
  for (i=0; i<LEN_1D; i++) {
      if (!found && a[i]==max_val) {
          first_index = i;
          found = 1;
      }
  }
  ```
  But this has a loop-carried dependency on `found` and `first_index`, not vectorizable.  

Better:  
- In Phase 1, also track the first index of max using a masked min:  
  ```
  max_val = a[0];
  int first_index = 0;
  for (i=1; i<LEN_1D; i++) {
      if (a[i] > max_val) {
          max_val = a[i];
          first_index = i;
      } else if (a[i] == max_val) {
          // keep first_index as is (first occurrence)
      }
  }
  ```
  This is the original algorithm — not vectorizable due to branching and dependencies.  

Thus, the split into Phase 1 + Phase 2 was meant to help, but Phase 2 is still not vectorized.  

**Conclusion**: The current optimized code fails vectorization for Phase 2, so there is **optimization opportunity** → FAIL.

The optimized code preserves semantics but does not achieve full vectorization (Phase 2 not vectorized).  
Therefore, further modification is needed → **FAIL**.

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Initialize array (vectorizable)
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        x = a[0];
        index = 0;
        
        // Phase 1: Find max value (vectorizable reduction)
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }

        // Phase 2: Find first index of max_val using masked min reduction
        int first_index = LEN_1D;

            int mask = (a[i] == max_val);
            int candidate = mask ? i : first_index;
            first_index = (candidate < first_index) ? candidate : first_index;
        }
        if (first_index == LEN_1D) first_index = 0;
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Test Case 1: LEN_1D=5, iterations=1**

- Optimized code returns: 1 + 3 + 1 = 5 ✓

**Semantic Issue Identified:**
The optimized code has a **semantic bug** when there are multiple occurrences of the maximum value. The source code finds the **last occurrence** of the maximum value (due to `if (a[i] > x)` condition - when equal, it doesn't update), while the optimized code finds the **first occurrence** (using `candidate < first_index`).

**Example demonstrating the issue:**
- LEN_1D=4, a = [3, 1, 3, 2]
- Source code: 
  - i=0: x=3, index=0
  - i=1: a[1]=1 < 3, no change
  - i=2: a[2]=3 == 3, not greater, no change
  - i=3: a[3]=2 < 3, no change
  - Result: index=0 (first occurrence)

{

    int index;

        x = a[0];
        index = 0;

Optimized code:
```c

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Phase 1: Vectorizable max reduction
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Vectorizable first index search using masked min
        int first_index = LEN_1D;
        float max_val_copy = max_val;  // Local copy for better optimization
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            // Branchless conditional update
            int mask = (a[i] == max_val_copy);
            int candidate = mask ? i : first_index;
            first_index = (candidate < first_index) ? candidate : first_index;
        }
        
        // Handle edge case (should never happen with our initialization)
        if (first_index == LEN_1D) first_index = 0;
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

```

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go through the steps systematically.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in Optimized Code**  
The optimized code compiles without errors (compiler feedback shows vectorization remarks, no error messages).  

---

**2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**  

**Source code logic:**  
- Initialization: `a[i] = (i * 7) % LEN_1D` (integer arithmetic, stored as float).  
- In each iteration `nl`:  
  1. Find the **maximum value** in array `a`.  
  2. Find the **first index** where that maximum occurs.  
  3. `chksum = x + (float) index` (unused except for possible side effects).  
- Returns `index + x + 1` (as float) from the **last iteration**’s `index` and `x`.  

**Optimized code logic:**  
- Initialization: same.  
- In each iteration `nl`:  
  1. Find `max_val` in `a`.  
  2. Create `found_indices[i] = (a[i] == max_val) ? i : LEN_1D`.  
  3. Find `first_index` as `min(found_indices[i])`.  
  4. If `first_index == LEN_1D`, set to 0.  
- Returns `(float)index + x + 1.0f`.  

**Potential semantic difference:**  
- In the source code, if multiple indices have the same max value, `index` is the **first** encountered during sequential search from `i = 0`.  
- In the optimized code, the `first_index` search also finds the first occurrence (by taking min of indices where `a[i] == max_val`).  
- However, there’s a subtlety: floating-point equality `a[i] == max_val`.  
  Since `max_val` is computed from the same array in floating-point, and `a[i]` values are integers stored as floats, equality should hold exactly for the same indices as in the source code’s `if (a[i] > x)` logic (where `x` starts as `a[0]` and updates only when strictly greater).  
  But: what if `max_val` appears multiple times? The source code’s `index` will be the **first** occurrence because it updates `index` only when `a[i] > x`, not when `a[i] == x`.  
  Wait — careful: The source code’s `x` starts at `a[0]`. If `a[0]` is the maximum, `x` never changes, so `index` stays 0 even if later elements equal `x`.  
  The optimized code’s `first_index` in that case will also be 0 (since `a[0] == max_val`).  
  If `a[0]` is not the maximum, say `a[2]` is max and `a[5]` also equals max, source code sets `index = 2` when first seeing `a[2] > x`, then later `a[5] == x` so no update. So `index = 2`.  
  Optimized code: `found_indices` will have `2` and `5`, min is `2`. Same result.  

So logically they match.  

But there’s a **critical bug** in optimized code:  
- In source code, `index` and `x` are updated together inside the same loop.  
- In optimized code, `max_val` is found in one pass, then `first_index` in another pass using `a[i] == max_val`.  
- This is fine **except** if `max_val` is `NaN`.  
  If `a` contains `NaN`, source code’s `if (a[i] > x)` is always false if `x` is `NaN` (because `NaN > anything` is false), so `x` stays `NaN`, `index` stays 0.  
  Optimized code: `max_val` computed via `if (val > max_val)` — if `max_val` starts as `NaN`, same issue: `val > NaN` false, so `max_val` stays `NaN` for whole pass. Then `a[i] == max_val` is false for all `i` (even `NaN == NaN` is false in IEEE 754), so `found_indices[i]` all `LEN_1D`, `first_index = LEN_1D`, then set to 0. So `index = 0`, `x = NaN`. Same result.  

Actually, with `NaN` present, both yield `index = 0`, `x = NaN`.  

But initialization `(i * 7) % LEN_1D` yields integers 0..LEN_1D-1, no `NaN`. So no problem in practice.  

So semantics match for valid inputs.  

Unit test says PASS, so likely fine.  

---

**3. Vectorization analysis**  

Compiler feedback shows:  
- Loop at line 10 (initialization) vectorized width 4.  
- Loop at line 20 (max reduction) vectorized width 4, interleaved count 4.  
- Loop at line 30 (found_indices fill) vectorized width 4, interleaved count 4.  
- Loop at line 36 (min index reduction) vectorized width 4, interleaved count 4.  

All loops vectorized.  

---

**4. Optimization opportunities**  

The optimized code uses **two separate passes** (max, then min-of-indices) instead of the original single pass.  
This increases memory traffic (allocates `found_indices[LEN_1D]` on stack each iteration — could be large if `LEN_1D` large).  

Better approach:  
- Single-pass vectorizable max reduction that also tracks **first index** of max:  
  Use two vectors: one for max values, one for max indices. On tie (value equal to current max), keep the earlier index (smaller index).  
  This avoids extra array and second pass.  

But the current code **is** vectorized, so technically passes requirement.  

However, the extra temporary array is unnecessary and could hurt performance for large LEN_1D.

Optimized code:
```c

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Phase 1: Vectorizable max reduction
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Vectorizable first index search using parallel min reduction
        // Store comparison results in a temporary array
        int found_indices[LEN_1D];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            found_indices[i] = (a[i] == max_val) ? i : LEN_1D;
        }
        
        // Find minimum index (first occurrence) using vectorizable reduction
        int first_index = LEN_1D;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            if (found_indices[i] < first_index) {
                first_index = found_indices[i];
            }
        }
        
        // Handle edge case
        if (first_index == LEN_1D) first_index = 0;
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

```

  2. Find the **first index** where that maximum occurs.

**Optimized code logic:**  
- Initialization: same.  
- In each iteration `nl`:  
  1. Find `max_val` in `a`.  
  2. Create `found_indices[i] = (a[i] == max_val) ? i : LEN_1D`.  
  3. Find `first_index` as `min(found_indices[i])`.

**Potential semantic difference:**  
- In the source code, if multiple indices have the same max value, `index` is the **first** encountered during sequential search from `i = 0`.

  But: what if `max_val` appears multiple times? The source code’s `index` will be the **first** occurrence because it updates `index` only when `a[i] > x`, not when `a[i] == x`.  
  Wait — careful: The source code’s `x` starts at `a[0]`. If `a[0]` is the maximum, `x` never changes, so `index` stays 0 even if later elements equal `x`.  
  The optimized code’s `first_index` in that case will also be 0 (since `a[0] == max_val`).  
  If `a[0]` is not the maximum, say `a[2]` is max and `a[5]` also equals max, source code sets `index = 2` when first seeing `a[2] > x`, then later `a[5] == x` so no update. So `index = 2`.  
  Optimized code: `found_indices` will have `2` and `5`, min is `2`. Same result.  

So logically they match.  

But there’s a **critical bug** in optimized code:  
- In source code, `index` and `x` are updated together inside the same loop.  
- In optimized code, `max_val` is found in one pass, then `first_index` in another pass using `a[i] == max_val`.  
- This is fine **except** if `max_val` is `NaN`.

  Optimized code: `max_val` computed via `if (val > max_val)` — if `max_val` starts as `NaN`, same issue: `val > NaN` false, so `max_val` stays `NaN` for whole pass. Then `a[i] == max_val` is false for all `i` (even `NaN == NaN` is false in IEEE 754), so `found_indices[i]` all `LEN_1D`, `first_index = LEN_1D`, then set to 0. So `index = 0`, `x = NaN`. Same result.  

Actually, with `NaN` present, both yield `index = 0`, `x = NaN`.  

But initialization `(i * 7) % LEN_1D` yields integers 0..LEN_1D-1, no `NaN`. So no problem in practice.  

So semantics match for valid inputs.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    else

Let’s re-evaluate:  
Original:  
`if (a[i] > x)` updates index.  
If `a[i] == x`, no update.  
Thus, first index of max is kept.  

Optimized:  
`first_index` updates if `is_greater` or `(is_equal && i < first_index)`.  
The second condition tries to find the smallest index among equal max values — same as original.  

But there’s a subtle bug:  
When `is_greater` is true, `first_index = i`.  
When `is_greater` is false but `is_equal` is true and `i < first_index`, `first_index = i`.

        x = max_val;

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

     a[i] = x + y;

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Single loop with vectorizable reduction for both max value and first index
        float max_val = a[0];
        int first_index = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            // Vectorizable max reduction
            int is_greater = val > max_val;
            // Update max_val using masking (branchless)
            max_val = is_greater ? val : max_val;
            // Update first_index: if we found a new max, use current index;
            // if equal to current max and first_index is still at initial position, update
            int is_equal = val == max_val;
            int is_initial = first_index == 0;
            int should_update = (is_greater) || (is_equal && is_initial && i == 0);
            first_index = should_update ? i : first_index;
        }
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

But the real bug: `is_initial = first_index == 0` — after first update, `is_initial` becomes false, so later equal max values won’t update `first_index`. But in the source, later equal max values don’t update index anyway (since condition is `>` not `>=`). So it matches.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Loop splitting: separate max value reduction from index finding
        float max_val = a[0];
        
        // First loop: find max value (pure reduction, vectorizable)
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Second loop: find first index where a[i] == max_val (vectorizable search)
        int first_index = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            if (a[i] == max_val) {
                first_index = i;
                break;  // First occurrence found
            }
        }
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

     }

   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**

   - Example pattern:  
     ```c

   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```

**Application Rules**:

     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;

     // Before reorder:

     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Loop splitting: separate max value reduction from index finding
        float max_val = a[0];
        
        // First loop: find max value (pure reduction, vectorizable)
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Second loop: find first index where a[i] == max_val
        // Remove early break to enable vectorization
        int found_index = 0;
        int found = 0; // 0 = not found yet, 1 = found
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            // Vectorizable comparison: check if this is the first occurrence of max_val
            int match = (a[i] == max_val) && !found;
            found_index = match ? i : found_index;
            found = match ? 1 : found;
        }
        
        index = found_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // First loop: find max value (pure reduction, vectorizable)
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Second loop: find first index where a[i] == max_val
        // Use vectorizable pattern without loop-carried dependency on found flag
        int first_index = LEN_1D; // Initialize with invalid index
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            // Create mask for equality and earlier position
            int is_match = (a[i] == max_val);
            int is_earlier = (i < first_index);
            // Update first_index using arithmetic instead of conditional branch
            first_index = is_match ? (is_earlier ? i : first_index) : first_index;
        }
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

## **3. Vectorization Analysis**

Compiler feedback:  
- First initialization loop: vectorized. ✅  
- First inner loop (max reduction): vectorized. ✅  
- Second inner loop (first index where a[i]==max_val): **not vectorized** (value used outside loop not identified as reduction).  

The second loop has a loop-carried dependency:  
`first_index` is updated conditionally based on `i < first_index`.  
This is a min reduction of indices where `a[i] == max_val`.  
But the compiler fails to recognize it as a reduction because of the complex condition.  

We can rewrite it as a pure min reduction:  
```
int first_index = LEN_1D;
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    if (a[i] == max_val) {
        first_index = (i < first_index) ? i : first_index;
    }
}
```
Even this has a dependency: `first_index` is both read and written in loop.  
But `min` reduction is associative: we can compute the minimum index where condition holds by:  
- Vectorize comparison to get a mask.  
- For each lane where mask is true, keep lane index, else keep large number.  
- Compute min across vector.  

We can help compiler by using OpenMP SIMD reduction or writing it differently:  
```
int first_index = LEN_1D;
#pragma omp simd reduction(min:first_index)
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    if (a[i] == max_val && i < first_index) {
        first_index = i;
    }
}
```
But the `i < first_index` part in condition makes it not a pure reduction across iterations if written like this — better to remove it:  
```
int first_index = LEN_1D;
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    if (a[i] == max_val) {
        first_index = i;
        break; // first found
    }
}
```
But `break` prevents vectorization.  

Better: find all indices where a[i]==max_val, then take min:  
We can vectorize the equality check and collect candidate indices, but min reduction on indices where mask is true is vectorizable:  
```
int first_index = LEN_1D;
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
    int match = (a[i] == max_val);
    int candidate = match ? i : LEN_1D;
    if (candidate < first_index) first_index = candidate;
}
```
This is a min reduction on `candidate`. Compiler might vectorize this.  

But current optimized code’s second loop is **not vectorized** according to feedback.  
Thus there is **optimization opportunity** (the second loop can be vectorized as a min reduction).

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

float s315_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a)
{
    // Vectorizable initialization
    for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
        a[i] = (float)((i * 7) % LEN_1D);
    }

    float x;
    int index;
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // First loop: find max value (pure reduction, vectorizable)
        float max_val = a[0];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            float val = a[i];
            if (val > max_val) {
                max_val = val;
            }
        }
        
        // Second loop: find first index where a[i] == max_val
        // Use min reduction pattern for vectorization
        int first_index = LEN_1D;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; ++i) {
            // Create mask for equality
            int match = (a[i] == max_val);
            // Candidate index: if match, use i; otherwise use LEN_1D (large value)
            int candidate = match ? i : LEN_1D;
            // Min reduction: find smallest candidate
            if (candidate < first_index) {
                first_index = candidate;
            }
        }
        
        index = first_index;
        x = max_val;
    }
    
    return (float)index + x + 1.0f;
}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis Report  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors.  

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  
The optimized code **does not** preserve the original semantics.  

**Reason**:  
- In the original code, when multiple elements have the same maximum value (`x`), the **last** index where that maximum occurs is stored (because the loop updates `index` whenever `a[i] >= x`).  
- In the optimized code, the first loop finds `max_val`, and the second loop finds the **first** index where `a[i] == max_val`.  
- This changes the final value of `index` when the maximum value appears more than once.  

**Example verification**:  
Let `LEN_1D = 5`, `iterations = 1`.  
Initialization:  
`a = [0, 2, 4, 1, 3]` (since `(i*7) % 5` yields `0, 2, 4, 1, 3`).  

- Original loop:  
  - `i=0`: `x=0`, `index=0`  
  - `i=1`: `a[1]=2 > 0` → `x=2`, `index=1`  
  - `i=2`: `a[2]=4 > 2` → `x=4`, `index=2`  
  - `i=3`: `a[3]=1` not greater  
  - `i=4`: `a[4]=3` not greater  
  Final: `x=4`, `index=2`.  

- Optimized loop:  
  First loop finds `max_val = 4`.  
  Second loop finds first `i` where `a[i]==4` → `i=2`.  
  Same result here, but not in general.  

Counterexample:  
Change `a` to `[4, 4, 3, 2, 1]`.  
- Original: last maximum at `i=1` → `index=1`.  
- Optimized: first maximum at `i=0` → `index=0`.  
Thus, semantics differ.  

**Conclusion**: The optimized code fails semantic equivalence.  

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Since semantics are incorrect, vectorization analysis is not required.  

### Final Result  
**FAIL** — The optimized code does not match the source code’s behavior.
